@@119987232 <h> OPINION <h> Some hard lessons for the DA from Robert Mugabe 's GNU <p> David Coltart <p> 13 January 2025 <p> David Coltart warns the party against repeating the errors made by the MDC in govt <p> Now that the dust has settled over the GNU and it has begun to settle on a reasonably functional governance path , South Africa is breathing a sigh of relief . Had the ANC chosen to form a ' doomsday ' alliance with the MK Party or the EFF -- or both -- we would by now be experiencing the consequences including human and capital flight and the beginnings of the Russification of the country . <p> But this is no time for complacency . I was part of the MDC that entered the Zimbabwean Government of National Unity in February 2009 . In many ways , the MDC got a better deal than the DA has cut with the ANC . The MDC 's leader , Morgan Tsvangirai was made Prime Minister , I was the Minister of Education , Sport and Culture and Tendai Biti was given the Finance Ministry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the MDC -- actually two parties , the MDC-M and the MDC-T - came out of the GNU poorly , as Zanu-PF used the GNU and the goodwill it generated internationally to hollow out the opposition and take the wind out of its sails . When the GNU was disbanded , Zanu-PF was stronger than ever and has managed by fair means and foul to stay in power ever since . <p> Here are some of the key lessons from our experience inside that GNU : <p> 1 . Assimilation of Opposition <p> Soon after the formation of the GNU in February 2009 and the swearing-in of Ministers , it became apparent that ZANU-PF would use the trappings of power to compromise MDC Ministers . In my case , one of the first acts of the Permanent Secretary of the Ministry of Education was to focus on the need for me to acquire a new Mercedez Benz vehicle . <p> --&gt; <p> This literally became the priority over the fact that some 8 000 schools were closed and over 100 000 teachers were on strike when I took office @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all other MDC Ministers succumbed and within a few months their public persona was no different to that of the ZANU-PF ministers . <p> Things went downhill from there . Several MDC Ministers themselves sought perks . For example , some Ministers insisted on being driven to the stairs of aircraft and insisted on emulating many of the practices of ZANU-PF Ministers to enhance their status . In the case of Morgan Tsvangirai , he was given a luxurious home in Harare which became the subject of considerable controversy . The use of all the trappings of power resulted in MDC Ministers being hard to distinguish from ZANU-PF Ministers by the end of the GNU in 2013 . <p> The stark difference between ZANU-PF leaders and MDC leaders prior to the GNU became fudged . As a result , those members of the electorate on the periphery of political parties , that is people who were not members of parties and had no real interest in parties , found very little to distinguish MDC Ministers from ZANU-PF Ministers which led to apathy in the August 2013 Election and a relatively @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ In terms of the agreement that led to the GNU Cabinet was to be chaired by President Mugabe , ( responsible for policy formulation ) , and a new body known as the Council of Ministers , chaired by Prime Minister Morgan Tsvangirai , was meant to be responsible for implementation of the policies agreed by Cabinet . The creation of the Council of Ministers in theory provided a significant opportunity for Prime Minister Morgan Tsvangirai and the MDC parties to exercise power but that opportunity was squandered . <p> The Council of Ministers was poorly run ; it rarely started on time , was often cancelled and there was inadequate follow up on its resolutions . It stood in stark contrast to Cabinet itself which was punctual and orderly . Ministers would never miss Cabinet meetings whereas they regularly missed Council of Ministers meetings . That was partly of course created by ZANU-PF Ministers who could not be held to account but very little was done to exercise discipline . As a result , within a few months of the commencement of the GNU , it became increasingly obvious @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decisions were taken there . As time went on the Council of Ministers became more and more ineffective . <p> 3 . Naivety <p> Whilst the two MDC Parties did not have the support they needed from SADC and the AU to force President Mugabe to allocate more influential Ministries , the fact of the matter is that the 2 MDC Parties were allocated what were termed " non-coercive , " but important , Ministries which included the Ministry of Finance , Education , Health and Industry &amp; Commerce . <p> --&gt; <p> Most MDC Ministers spent 5 years stabilising their respective Ministries and their relevant spheres of influence while at the same time , ZANU-PF focused on consolidating its own power . By 2013 the economy had been stabilized , there were drugs back in hospitals , teachers were back teaching , hyper-inflation had been tackled and as a result , there was far less economic pain for people and far less reason for them to exercise a protest vote against ZANU-PF as had happened in the March 2008 Election . ZANU-PF cynically allowed this situation and allowed the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ MDC any headway in seeking to influence the coercive Ministries held by ZANU-PF . <p> As a result , ZANU-PF 's control over the Military , the Police , the Judiciary and the Prison Service remained unaltered . They retained all the levers of power while the MDC somewhat naively applied all its focus on service delivery to people . Not that that was wrong but on reflection , it is obvious that the leverage provided by running service Ministries well was never exercised to effectively change the coercive structures of power . <p> 4 . Miscalculations around the Constitution <p> Arguably the most important facet of the GNU was the Constitutional reform process . Whilst the new Constitution which was endorsed by a Referendum in March 2013 was a significant improvement on the Lancaster House Constitution , the deficiencies in the 2013 Constitution illustrate the naivety and lack of tactical nouse displayed by the 2 MDCs parties during the GNU . <p> --&gt; <p> Relatively junior people were delegated to head the MDCs ' Constitutional teams . Senior lawyers such as Tendai Biti and Welshman Ncube had their work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they were involved in the deadlock-breaking appeals committee , they were not in a position to fully devote their energies to the drafting of the Constitution itself . <p> That situation was compounded by the fact that on some of the key debates regarding the constitution , the considerable leverage that the MDC Parties had was not exercised . For example , when it came to the key argument regarding the separation of powers , and in particular the reduction of the Executive 's powers , the 2 MDC parties made some key errors of judgment . <p> As a result , the Office of the President was left with vast powers and no real movement was made towards moving from a Westminster system to , for example , a proportional representation system . There was insufficient consultation and strategic planning which went on within the 2 MDCs , something exacerbated by the fact that the 2 MDCs remained separate political parties and consequently , there was very little constructive discussion and strategic planning between the 2 parties to come up with agreed positions . <p> Very often the MDC @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ MDC T and ZANU-PF parties rather than the 2 MDC parties presenting an agreed unified front to the ZANU-PF position . That situation was further exacerbated by overconfidence within the MDC T which thought that it would easily win the next election , and which did not share the MDC M 's disquiet regarding the substantial powers which were left in the hands of the Executive . <p> 5 . Little Reform to Key Institutions <p> Whilst the new constitution recognised key Institutions such as the Judiciary and Media and whilst the new constitution had new measures , which in theory enhanced the independence of Institutions such as the Judiciary and the Media , in practical terms very little was done to reform those institutions . <p> In the case of the judiciary , there was hardly any noticeable reform . The existing Judges remained . Although there were key changes to the constitution to strengthen the independence of the Judiciary there were inadequate safeguards to protect those provisions . Furthermore , inadequate measures were taken to entrench the independence of the Judiciary . But , perhaps the most significant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effect change in the composition of the Judiciary during the 5 years of the GNU through the swearing-in of new Judges who had no obvious party affiliation . The net result was that by the end of the GNU , there were theoretical changes to the Constitutional structure of the Judiciary but very little practical change , and even the Constitutional protections were easy to amend , as has in fact happened . <p> The same applies to the media . Whilst there is one provision in the Constitution , ( Section 62(4) ) which tried to enforce the notion of an independent state media , there was little else in the Constitution focused on to practically allow the development of a vibrant and independent media in Zimbabwe . By the end of the GNU , there were still no independent radio or television stations and all the television and radio stations in 2013 remained either in the direct or indirect control of ZANU-PF . <p> As a result of this , both the Judiciary and the Media since 2013 have remained largely within the control of ZANU-PF and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have made both institutions more partisan than they have ever been . <p> The same applies to other institutions such as universities and Constitutional Commissions such as the Electoral Commission , Human Rights Commission and Anti-Corruption Commission . All of these bodies whilst recognised by the Constitution as " independent " are less independent now than their equivalent bodies were in 2008 . <p> Lessons for the DA <p> Whilst the current situation in South Africa is substantially different to the conditions in Zimbabwe between 2009 and 2013 there are some lessons to be learnt as follows : <p> DA Ministers need to resist the trappings of power and should rather eschew as many of the current Cabinet benefits as possible . Much of this is a matter of public perception but a collective effort should be made towards setting a different standard for Ministers in terms of entourages , cavalcades and many of the symbols of power which irritate the general public and give the impressions that there is no difference between DA and ANC Cabinet members . <p> 2 . I have been impressed by the apparent work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ office and this must be continued . The GNU is a marathon , not a sprint , and it is necessary to set out clear annual goals for t4he DA Ministries together with overarching goals for the life of the GNU . Then a vigorous public media campaign must be adopted , going beyond the individual Ministers themselves ( some of whom are better at social media than others ) to publicise and mark those goals and their achievement . The corollary to this is that the DA must not hesitate to do all in its power remove and replace non performing Ministers . <p> 3 . The DA needs to pay attention to areas which they do not control , particularly with regard to the safeguarding of the electoral process , the independence of the media , judiciary , civic bodies and universities . It is critical that they do all in their power to build these institutions whose independence should be enhanced during the tenure of the GNU . <p> 4 . An increasing focus should be on SADC and its member states . Whilst foreign policy concerns @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ South Africa ultimately will depend on whether a majority of SDAC countries become democratic or not . In other words in my view the primary foreign policy goal should be the democratisation of region rather than international issues such as , for example , Ukraine and Gaza . Whilst these international issues obviously remain important my view is that the DA should focus on a strident foreign policy which will , for example , enhance free , fair , lawful and independently run elections in the region and see the restoration of the SADC Tribunal .

The other countries which voted against resolutions in this period were Algeria , Bolivia , Burundi , Egypt , Eritrea , Iraq , Kyrgyzstan , Philippines , Somalia , and Venezuela . <h> Issues repeatedly covered in the 18 UNHRC sessions <p> This is a very brief summary of some of the many issues that were the subject matter of the resolutions . What could this mean in the long term for former opposition parties like the Democratic Alliance ? We asked David Coltart , a former minister in Robert Mugabe 's Government of National Unity , to share some of the lessons learned in Zimbabwe . Welcome , Mr. Coltart . <p> David Coltart ( 00:30.084 ) : Great being with you , Chris . <p> Chris Steyn ( 00:33.516 ) : Sir , how did ZANU-PF use the Government of National Unity in Zimbabwe to hollow out the opposition and become stronger than ever ? <p> David Coltart ( 00:45.464 ) : They were very cynical right from the outset . Of course , they were supported by President Mbeki , who enabled them to retain what we term the coercive ministries . They kept defense , control of the judiciary , the electoral process , the police , and all the main instruments of power in the country . Although the opposition MDC had some significant ministries , such as finance , they never really got close to controlling the levers of power . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ years of the GNU , particularly in the ministries the MDC controlled -- finance , education , health , and the like -- ZANU did n't yield a centimeter of their actual hold on power . <p> By the 2013 election , they controlled the police , the army , the media , and the electoral process . In effect , they had almost total control over the country . They used that to subvert the 2013 election , undermine the opposition , and retain absolute power . <p> Chris Steyn ( 02:13.444 ) : And then they traded on the good performance of the former opposition ministers in that GNU , did they not ? <p> David Coltart ( 02:21.838 ) : Well , of course . I was Minister of Education . When I started , 8,000 schools were still closed , and 110,000 teachers were on strike . Public examinations written the previous November had n't even been marked by February 2009 . It was an absolutely chaotic situation in education . By the end of the five years , we had all teachers back at work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for every subject and child . We even brought 15,000 more teachers back into the system and stabilized the education sector . ZANU benefited greatly from that . <p> The same applied to finance . Inflation dropped to single digits , and the economy grew over 10% in two years of the inclusive government . This took the sting out of the suffering . <p> In contrast , in South Africa , the ANC performed poorly in elections because people suffered , particularly those on the periphery . By 2013 in Zimbabwe , the situation had improved to the extent that people did n't feel the same need to vote for the opposition . Combined with ZANU 's control over media , the judiciary , and the electoral process , this allowed them to dominate the elections . <p> Chris Steyn ( 04:07.950 ) : So what advice do you have for the Democratic Alliance and other former opposition parties in South Africa 's Government of National Unity to avoid the same fate ? <p> David Coltart ( 04:19.086 ) : There are obvious differences between Zimbabwe 's GNU from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The MDC was at a huge disadvantage compared to the DA . The MDC was split into two factions , neither of which was well-organized , while the DA strikes me as disciplined and well-structured . <p> However , there are lessons for the DA . One critical issue is that the MDC contributed to its downfall through some actions . For instance , MDC ministers quickly adopted ZANU-PF 's operating methods . They embraced the trappings of power -- luxury cars , high-end lifestyles -- and Morgan Tsvangirai , the Prime Minister , moved into a new , luxurious house . By the end of five years , many in the electorate could n't distinguish MDC ministers from ZANU-PF ministers . It appeared we were in it for the perks , not for real change . <p> Secondly , we were naive . I , for instance , focused entirely on education , which was the right thing to do , but I neglected other critical areas like constitutional reform . While we succeeded in our ministries , we did n't challenge ZANU 's consolidation of power over the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2013 , ZANU had entrenched its dominance . <p> For South Africa , the DA and IFP must strike a balance . They must deliver effectively in their ministries to demonstrate competence and inspire confidence . However , they must also remain vigilant , ensuring the ANC does n't consolidate control over civic bodies , universities , the judiciary , and critically , the media . <p> Chris Steyn ( 08:23.17 ) What are the strongest parallels that you are seeing currently between what happened in Zimbabwe 's GNU under Robert Mugabe and what is happening in the first months of the GNU in South Africa ? <p> David Coltart ( 08:36.556 ) Well , I 've been quite impressed by what I 've seen . The parallels are that I 've seen DA ministers , for example -- if you look at Home Affairs -- it appears as if a lot of the backlog in applications has been dealt with . That 's very impressive . I 'm not so sure about education because , although I think , if I 'm correct , the pass rate might have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ level . So it 's hard at this stage , and it 's too early , quite frankly , to comment on how well people are doing . <p> I read a story , I think last week , where it was clarified that DA ministers were not taking new vehicles , that they simply took over old vehicles , and all of that is to be commended . But there 's quite a long way to go . It is important that they maintain that standard . <p> You know , the public are tired , I think , of the huge cavalcades , the huge entourages , the luxurious standards . This was certainly the case in Zimbabwe , and I suspect it 's the case in South Africa , where ministers get into these positions and suddenly their lifestyle transforms dramatically . It 's critical that the DA guards against that so they retain the goodwill of the electorate . And it 's a real opportunity for them to give a different example to the electorate . <p> I often use the example of when I once @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at his meeting with me on a bicycle . Now , it might be difficult getting on a bicycle in the center of Pretoria or Cape Town , but the principle is the same . And one often sees these Scandinavian ministers using public transport . Now , I 'm not a socialist . I 'm not advocating ... <p> David Coltart ( 10:53.87 ) ... but I think that what has to be conveyed to the electorate is that DA and IFP ministers understand the notion of servant leadership -- that they are there to serve , not to be served . And that needs to be a constant theme throughout their time in office . <p> Chris Steyn ( 11:14.072 ) Meanwhile , what do you make of former President Jacob Zuma 's MKP ? How big a threat do you think that party could be to the GNU ? <p> David Coltart ( 11:23.598 ) I do n't see it as a major threat as long as the GNU works and delivers to the South African people . <p> I think if the good start is continued @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to grow again -- if you attract investment , tackle crime , and continue addressing the issues at Eskom -- if more money is spent on health , education , and housing , and standards improve , that will negate any threat from MK and Jacob Zuma . <p> But if the DA and the IFP lapse into this notion that they hold these positions as of right and do n't have to deliver , then they will become very vulnerable to the populism of MK and the EFF , to a lesser extent . <p> Chris Steyn ( 12:32.567 ) Now , there are people who feel that since the Democratic Alliance and other parties have gone into government , they are not standing up for the issues they used to fight for before and that voters elected them to fight for . I do n't know if you 've noticed that . While the agreement , set to last 42 days , allows for the release of prisoners on both sides and the return of displaced people to their decimated homes , it can not erase the devastating humanitarian crisis inflicted on Gaza . <p> This agreement , received by Hamas as a victory against Israel 's " war of extermination , " will allow for desperately needed aid to reach Palestinian victims who have endured systematic starvation and medical blockades . The International Court of Justice ( ICJ ) ruled over a year ago that withholding humanitarian aid in such a manner constitutes extermination -- a verdict Israel has flagrantly ignored . <p> This blockade has left Gaza 's population , particularly women and children , vulnerable to deliberate and coordinated suffering . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ phase , includes the exchange of 33 hostages -- women , children , and the elderly -- for Palestinian prisoners held in Israeli jails . During this period , Israeli forces will retreat to the east allowing displaced Palestinians to begin returning to their homes , many of which have been reduced to rubble . Additionally , hundreds of aid trucks carrying essential supplies , including food , medical equipment , and fuel , will be permitted entry into Gaza each day . <p> --&gt; <p> Negotiations for the second phase , scheduled to start on the 16th day , will aim to secure the release of the remaining hostages and a full withdrawal of Israeli forces from Gaza . The final phase envisions the reconstruction of Gaza , a process that could take years , as well as the repatriation of any remaining hostages ' bodies . <p> While these steps offer a glimpse of hope , they do not erase the catastrophic destruction inflicted upon Gaza or the genocidal intent behind Israel 's actions . <p> Since the beginning of Israel 's latest genocidal campaign in October 2023 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them women and children -- despite official figures downplaying the true scale of the carnage . The aim is clear : to erase Palestinian history , identity , and existence from their ancestral land . <p> The EFF continues to stand resolutely with the Palestinian people in their legitimate campaign to reclaim their land and secure their right to life by any means necessary . We also continue to categorically reject the framing of their struggle as terrorism and instead recognise it as a battle for freedom against a colonial occupation that has endured for far too long . <p> --&gt; <p> Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and the Israeli Occupation Forces ( IOF ) are war criminals . Even as the ceasefire was being finalised , Israeli forces continued their bombardment yesterday , killing 82 civilians so far in Gaza . This hypocrisy highlights the ongoing violence that Palestinians face daily , even in moments of supposed reprieve . <p> The EFF salutes the resilience of the Palestinian people and Hamas , and calls on the international community to hold Israel accountable for its crimes against humanity . @@120147398 <h> OPINION SA 's pro-rights stance often falls short when theneed for protection of dignity arises at home <p> Workers continue their operation at the Stilfontein mine shaft to rescue illegal miners who have been underground for months . Image : REUTERS/IHSAAN HAFFEJEE . <p> As a relatively young nation , one of the attributes we pride ourselves on is our ubuntu . We are a people who -- partly because of our traumatic history and what others did for us in those dark hours of need -- are always among the first to demonstrate human solidarity in times of tragedy . <p> As most of the world celebrates a historic ceasefire that would hopefully be the first step towards lasting peace in Gaza and other parts of Palestine , SA 's vocal role in trying to stop the bombardments that had cost thousands of lives will not go unnoticed . <p> In terms of the deal , Israel will gradually withdraw its forces from Gaza and release Palestinian detainees from its prisons in return for the freeing of Israeli hostages kidnapped by Hamas in October 2023 . <p> Much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ go to the Egyptian , Qatari and US mediators who facilitated protracted negotiations between the Israeli government and Hamas . Others would even attribute the breakthrough to US president-elect Donald Trump who , since his electoral victory in November , has been threatening that " all hell will break loose " in the Middle East if Hamas failed to release hundreds of Israeli hostages by Monday , the date of his inauguration . <p> However , there 's an argument to be made that SA 's intervention -- which included approaching the International Court of Justice ( ICJ ) to demand that Israel stop what SA and others have described as genocide -- played a big role in forcing Benjamin Netanyahu 's government to the negotiating table . <p> Many a commentator and activist , over the past year , has expressed a sense of pride over SA 's courage to stand up to Israel at the ICJ and demand that mass killings and the destruction of people 's homes in Gaza be stopped . <p> In the eyes of the international community , especially the so-called Global South , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ruins of apartheid to become a dependable champion for human rights . <p> Our constitution , whose bill of rights emphasises everyone 's right to life as well as their " inherent dignity and the right to have their dignity respected and protected " is celebrated by all the progressives across the world . Yet , we continue to suffer tragedies , some self-inflicted , that often leave one wondering if this reputation of a world champion of human rights is deserved . <p> Preventable deaths of poor schoolchildren in pit toilets , Life Esidimeni , the Marikana massacre -- these are the tragedies that should not be occurring in the type of society we claim to be . But they do . <p> At the time of writing this column , Sowetan and all other news agencies were reporting that 78 bodies and 246 survivors had been recovered from an abandoned gold mine in Stilfontein , North West . The figures are staggering , suggesting -- as the Business Day rightly put it yesterday morning -- that SA is " standing on the cusp of one of the worst @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a shock to anyone . <p> The tragic drama has been slowly unfolding in front of our eyes for years now , as the mining industry and law-enforcement agencies failed to deal effectively with illicit mining -- hence allowing it to develop into an extensive multi-million enterprise controlled by armed criminal gangs and gold trading syndicates . <p> For years , authorities have mostly looked the other way -- accepting the so-called zama zamas as a fact of economic life . But as the practice grew into a large network that is said to be siphoning tens of billions of rand from the country each year -- and communities neighbouring abandoned mines started complaining about violent crime associated with heavily armed gangs of zama zamas -- the authorities could not ignore the problem any further . <p> However , instead of addressing the underlining factors that draw so many South Africans , Zimbabweans , Basotho , Mozambicans and Malawians to such a dangerous activity -- factors such as high levels of unemployment in the region , porous borders and lack of clear regulations for artisanal mining -- the government responded @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the known exits to the abandoned mines does help in combating the crime by , as one minister ( in ) famously put it , " smoking out " and arresting the illegal miners -- it hardly addresses the problem . Those who have been " smoked out " , arrested and -- if they are undocumented immigrants -- deported would simply come back a few months down the line to do it again . More deaths would follow . A vicious cycle . <p> Undocumented immigrants or not , it should shame all of us that it has taken a court of law -- at the instigation of human rights activists and the Stilfontein community -- for our government to engage in a humanitarian rescue mission . <p> When the full extent of the tragedy finally weighs on us -- and the " criminal activity " argument no longer sounds like enough justification for not acting early -- we will be haunted by the same question that has haunted us since Marikana : Where was the ubuntu that is supposed to undergird our post-apartheid nation ?

@@120257810 <h> Israel won only the body count ... <h> One thing is crystal clear about the state of the Middle East following the implementation of a ceasefire yesterday between ... <p> A man walks past destroyed and damaged buildings at the site of an overnight Israeli airstrike . Picture : AFP <p> One thing is crystal clear about the state of the Middle East following the implementation of a ceasefire yesterday between Israel and Hamas to pause the carnage in Gaza . And that is : this is by no means over . <p> The Israeli military may have razed most of Gaza , leaving it looking like Germany at the end of the World War II , but it did not succeed either in destroying Hamas , or in forcing the Palestinians in Gaza to abandon their support for the movement . <p> Certainly , the Israelis may have won the body count ... even if they discount the Gaza authority 's assertion that 46 000 people perished since 7 October 2023 , then thousands of people , including civilians and children , died in the territory . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's summation of the liberation struggle in Rhodesia : Ian Smith 's military may have won the body count , but they lost the war ... <p> What the brutal retaliation did against Gaza -- and how can you not describe it as a genocide ? -- was create legions of angry people right across the Middle East who will not rest until Israel is destroyed . How long can the Israelis try to hold back that tide of history ? <p> At the same time , thanks must be offered up for the release of the hostages held by Hamas , as well as a thought in memory of those civilians slaughtered in the surprise attacks . How can you not call that terrorism ? <p> There must be a commitment from both sides to talking to each other and this should be bolstered by the international community and particularly the Arab community in the region . @@120266547 <h> DOCUMENTS <h> Israel-Gaza resolution cost UCT R750m - Adam Mendelsohn <p> Supplementary affidavit says council was not informed of amounts of donor funding being put at risk <p> IN THE HIGH COURT OF SOUTH AFRICA ( WESTERN CAPE DIVISION , CAPE TOWN ) <p> Case No. : 18465/24 <p> In the matter between : <p> ADAM MENDELSOHN <p> and <p> COUNCIL OF THE UNIVERSITY OF CAPE TOWN NORMAN ARENDSE N.O. <p> --&gt; <p> PERSONS LISTED IN ANNEXURE ' A ' UNIVERSITY OF CAPE TOWN <p> First Applicant <p> First Respondent Second Respondent <p> Third to Thirty-First Respondents Thirty-Second Respondent <p> --&gt; <p> AMENDED NOTICE OF MOTION <p> PLEASE TAKE NOTICE that the applicants will apply to this Honourable Court for an order in the following terms : <p> 1 . Declaring that the decisions of the First Respondent taken on 22 June 2024 , to pass two resolutions regarding the Gaza conflict are unlawful , unconstitutional , and invalid , and are reviewed and set aside . <p> 2 . Directing that- <p> --&gt; <p> 2.1 . The Second Respondent , in his personal capacity , pay a percentage to be determined by this Court of the applicant 's costs on an attorney and client scale , including the costs of two counsel on Scale C. <p> 2.2 . The Sixth Respondent , the Ninth Respondent , and the Thirty-First Respondent , in their personal capacity , pay in their personal capacities a percentage to be determined by this Court of the applicant 's costs on an attorney and client scale , including the costs of two counsel on Scale C. <p> 2.3 . Those members of the First Respondent who voted to oppose this application on 19 October 2024 pay in their personal capacities a percentage to be determined by this Court of the applicant 's costs on an attorney and client scale , including the costs of two counsel on Scale C. <p> 2.4 . The First and Thirty-Second Respondents pay , jointly and severally , any remainder of the applicant 's costs on an attorney and client scale , including the costs of two counsel on Scale C. <p> --&gt; <p> 3 . Granting the applicant such further and/or alternative relief as this Honourable Court deems fit . <p> TAKE NOTICE FURTHER that the application will proceed on the terms set out in the notice of motion dated 23 August 2024 . <p> 1 . I am the applicant in this matter . I deposed to the founding affidavit dated 22 August 2024 . <p> 2 . The facts contained in this affidavit fall within my knowledge , save where the contrary is stated or appears from the context . They are true and correct . Where I make submissions of law , I do so on the advice of my legal representatives . <p> 3 . I use the abbreviations and definitions of the founding affidavit in this affidavit . <p> I stand by the allegations in my founding affidavit , and this affidavit should be read alongside the founding affidavit . <p> A. INTRODUCTION <p> 4 . This is a supplementary founding affidavit delivered in terms of rule 53(4) of the Uniform Rules of Court . It supplements my founding affidavit , based on a consideration of the contents of the ( inaccurate and incomplete ) record filed by Council and relevant factual developments that have occurred since instituted this application . <p> 6 . First , since I delivered my founding affidavit , I learned that members of Council 's Executive Committee ( Exco ) deliberately failed to disclose relevant information at the Council meeting of 22 June 2024 . <p> 6.1 . On 23 May 2024 , Exco met . Exco 's members at the time were the Chair of Council ( Mr Arendse ) , as well as Ms Barsel , Mr Campbell , Mr Isaacs , Mr Khorommbi , Professor Ntusi , Ms Yach , and the Vice ? Chancellor ( then Professor Reddy ) . They were all present . <p> 6.2 . At this meeting , the Vice-Chancellor informed Exco that one donor ( the Donald Gordon Foundation , or DGF ) considered UCT in breach of a contract of donation , and another donor ( the Dell Foundation ) was suspending funding to UCT , because of Senate 's resolution to boycott academics affiliated to the IDF . These donations cumulatively amount to many millions of rands , with the DGF donation alone totalling R200 million . <p> 6.3 . So , the members of Exco knew that Senate 's resolution , if endorsed by Council , could cost UCT over R200 million in donations . The Chair , in particular , stressed the " importance of keeping discussions going " ( i.e. with donors ) , and that Senate 's resolution would become final only if adopted by the Council . <p> 6.4 . I attach the minutes of the May Exco meeting as SFA1 . <p> 6.5 . All the Exco members were present at Council 's meeting on 22 June 2024 , but according to the minutes Mr Isaacs and Professor Ntusi had left the meeting by the time the impugned resolutions came up for discussion . <p> 6.6 . During the Council meeting , while the question of passing the impugned resolutions was being debated , members of Council expressly asked about the potential implications of doing so for UCT ; raised the question of how a decision of this magnitude could be made without an analysis of possible consequences ; said that it was necessary to think about what the implications would be for UCT if Council were to adopt the resolutions ; and said that it would be the height of irrationality to adopt a motion without knowing what the consequences of doing so are . Specifically , the possibility that UCT would lose funding was raised . <p> 6.7 . None of the Exco members present disclosed to Council that the resolutions , if passed , could imminently cost the university more than R200 million , despite their knowledge of this fact . None of them revealed to Council the information they had about DGF and the Dell Foundation . <p> 6.8 . They failed to disclose this deliberately , in bad faith , and in breach of their fiduciary duties . <p> 6.9 . Exco members understood that the financial implications of the resolutions were relevant to Council 's decision . Members of Council expressly asked about the implications for the university if Council adopted the resolution . But the Exco members sat silent , allowing Council members to vote on a resolution without saying that doing so may very well imminently cost the university over R200 million . <p> 7 . In the report of 19 November 2024 prepared by Justice Kriegler , Professor Mosia and Ms Pillay SC into changes made to the Wilgenhof Panel 's report to the Council of the University of Stellenbosch about the possible closure of the Wilgenhof residence , the following is said ( at paragraph 66.1 ) : " A chairperson should always disclose significant circumstances in the interests of transparency and good governance . " In the case of UCT , not only the Chair of Council , but also the other members of Exco who were present during the relevant part of the Council meeting of 22 June 2024 , failed in this obvious duty . Their deliberate non-disclosure of material facts suffices to set aside the resolutions . <p> 8 . Secondly , Council 's decision has jeopardised the donation of a hospital to UCT . <p> 8.1 . Dr Susan Tager , who is a neurologist and the chief executive officer of the Wits Donald Gordon Medical Centre ( WDGMC ) , has deposed to a supporting affidavit , which will be filed along with this affidavit . She is a trustee of the DGF . <p> 8.2 . In her affidavit , Dr Tager explains that the DGF donated R120 million to the University of Witwatersrand in 2002 to establish Africa 's first ( and only ) private academic hospital . The hospital services patients predominantly from Gauteng . Since its establishment , the hospital has generated a further approximately R550 million in profit that has been used to train medical specialists and sub-specialists . The market value of the hospital is between R500 and R600 million today . <p> 8.3 . As is apparent from Dr Tager 's evidence , the WDGMC is a resounding success story . It trains much-needed medical specialists . It provides world-class medical care , including for those who can not afford it . It funds itself and uses its profits to train doctors . <p> 8.4 . The DGF intended to establish another hospital , modelled on the WDGMC , at UCT . In 2023 and 2024 , the DGF was in serious discussions with UCT . The DGF intended either to buy the current UCT academic hospital ( which is not owned by the university ) for UCT , or to provide funds to UCT to establish an entirely new hospital replicating the WDGMC . <p> 8.5 . The DGF was looking to donate R400 to R500 million to UCT to this end . <p> 8.6 . The discussions between the DGF and UCT have broken down as a direct result of Council 's resolutions . Given Council 's breach of contract with the DGF , the DGF can not continue working with UCT . <p> 8.7 . The point is a simple one : Council had no idea of this profound ramification when it passed its resolutions . It failed to consider - entirely - how passing the resolutions could cost UCT half a billion rand and a new hospital which would serve as a training facility for doctors . The loss is not only to UCT , but also to healthcare more generally . <p> 8.8 . Council 's failure to consider this plainly relevant factor suffices to set aside its decision . <p> 8.9 . Council 's failure speaks to a lapse in governance of severe proportions . Council was obliged to investigate and consider the ramifications its decision may have for the university . Had it conducted even a cursory inquiry , it would have learned that its decision would jeopardise one of the most significant donations to UCT in recent years ( and a donation aimed at serving the broader Western Cape ) . Council members breached their fiduciary duties to UCT by passing the resolutions in total , and indeed wilful , ignorance of this consequence . <p> 9 . Thirdly , since I launched this application , Council has acted contrary to its duties of good governance , its duties to this Court - and in fact has conceded the merits of this application . <p> 9.1 . One , Council has refused to provide a complete rule 53 record . <p> Council persistently refused to provide a recording or transcript of its meeting of 22 June 2024 , despite tendering that transcript to me ( which tender was accepted ) and despite having no basis whatsoever on which to withhold that transcript from this Court . In fact , Council decided not to disclose a transcript or recording of its meeting . Council has a number of lawyers among its members . It has attorneys and counsel advising it . It Is inconceivable , in the light of legal authority on the duty of discovery that rests on a public body whose decision is being challenged , that Council could have thought that it was entitled not to disclose the transcript or recording . It has only recently provided a transcript , as I shall describe below , but names of speakers have been redacted . <p> 9.2 . Two , Council has already decided to oppose this application . This decision is inexplicable . Council decided to oppose this application before filing a rule 53 record and before seeing my supplementary affidavit . Council , moreover , decided to oppose this application despite a large minority of members voting against the resolutions and against opposing this application . Worse still , when called upon to explain why it opposes this application before seeing my final grounds of review , and despite discord among its members , Council refused to give any reasons . <p> 9.3 . Three - and this should be the end of the matter - Council conceded in correspondence that it failed to consider relevant documents at its meeting of 22 June 2024 . The Chair of Council purported to file a rule 53 record including numerous documents that appeared not to have been before Council when Council passed the resolutions . The Chair explained that all these documents were " relevant " to Council 's decision . After Council had ratified the steps previously taken by the Chair and thereby endorsed the rule 53 record as filed by him , I wrote to Council to ask which of the " relevant " documents filed were before it when Council passed the resolutions . The answer : only one page of the 101 pages filed by the Chair was before Council . The implication : Council has conceded that it failed to consider relevant documents . This suffices to set aside the decision . <p> 10 . Fourthly , UCT has suspended me from my position as Head of the History Department because I brought this application . UCT is " investigating " whether , by bringing this application , I acted " unprofessionally " and " neglected " my leadership duties . The point , for the purposes of this application , is simple : UCT has plainly committed itself to censoring freedom of expression and academic freedom - so much so that it has retaliated against the mere bringing of an application that asserts the constitutional right to freedom of expression . <p> 11 . Finally , this case has very little to do with Israel and the legality of its conduct in Gaza . This case concerns Council 's statutory duty ( " must " ) to govern UCT . It concerns the question of whether Council acted in the best interests of the university . It is concerned with whether Council 's process leading to the resolutions accords with the principle of legality , good governance , and fiduciary duties owed to UCT . It concerns whether Council can ever limit academic freedom in the manner it has purported to do in this matter . These are issues of principle and process . They do not turn on the facts particular to Israel , Gaza , and the Middle East . <p> 12.3 . How the factual developments and the rule 53 record support the grounds of review advanced in the founding affidavit and introduce further grounds of review . <p> 12.4 . Remedy and costs , including personal costs . <p> B. RELEVANT FACTUAL DEVELOPMENTS <p> ( i ) August and September 2024 <p> 13 . I initiated this application on 23 August 2024 . <p> 14 . On 29 August 2024 , UCT ( as the thirty-second respondent ) appointed Fairbridges Wertheim Becker ( FWB ) as its attorneys . <p> 15 . On 2 September 2024 , Mr Kessler Perumalsamy ( the twenty-sixth respondent ) filed a notice to abide by this Court 's decision . <p> 16 . On 5 September 2024 , four Council members wrote to the Chairperson ( SFA2 ) . Their letter is destructive of any possible defence to this application . <p> 16.1 . At some date prior , Council members had been informed that legal representatives had been appointed to provide Council with an opinion on my application . <p> 16.2 . The letter reveals that Council itself did not decide to appoint legal representatives : that decision appears to have been taken unilaterally by the Chair . The letter explains that Council did not have an opportunity to consider , inter a ! ia , the cost implications of appointing lawyers , the grounds of review in my application , who should be briefed ( if anyone ) , the scope of the brief , and the instructions in the brief . <p> 16.3 . The Chair appears to have decided all these important issues himself , without consulting Council . He seems to have informed Council that it will meet after 30 September 2024 , by which time Council should have the opinion from the briefed legal representatives . <p> 16.4 . The letter includes a request for three documents that I alleged in my founding affidavit Council had failed to consider before passing the resolutions : the two legal opinions on the Council 's powers ( para 54 ) and the DGF donor contract ( para 52.5 ) . The letter confirms - unequivocally - that Council failed to consider those materially relevant documents . Council members were unaware of their existence . The decision to pass the resolutions can be set aside without more . <p> 16.5 . The letter also includes a request for the brief provided to Council 's legal representatives and for the contents of the rule 53 record UCT intends to file . <p> 16.6 . Council members had requested these documents previously from the Chair , but he failed to provide them . This letter was a repeat of the request for these documents . The letter records that the Chair had previously refused to . disclose these documents to Council members because they are " confidential " . Again , this confirms that Council failed to consider these relevant documents before passing the resolution . <p> 16.7 . Council members expressed grave concerns about the process leading up to passing the resolutions . They in fact confirm my grounds of review ; they allege that the process was irrational and unlawful . <p> 16.8 . With respect to the legal opinions , they record that the failure to inform Council of these constituted " a serious governance failure " that prevented members from exercising their fiduciary duties . They explain that whether the legal opinions were withheld deliberately or in good faith is irrelevant : in either event Council acted unlawfully by not considering the opinions . <p> 16.9 . As for the donor agreement , the letter reveals the startling fact of which I have made mention above . On 23 May 2024 , Exco met and discussed the fact that if Council passed the resolutions , UCT could lose the R200 million donation made under the donor agreement . When Council members inquired about the financial implications of the resolutions at the meeting of 22 June 2024 , the Exco members present said nothing . They failed to disclose the financial implications of the resolutions , despite knowing that UCT could lose ( at least ) R200 million in donations alone . <p> 16.10 . As it turns out , the number is at present between R250 million and R300 million , aside from the donation of a hospital by DGF . I return to this below . <p> 16.11 . The crucial point is this : on these members ' version , my application should succeed . Their letter reveals that there was a serious lapse in governance leading up to the passing of the resolutions . There were material non-disclosures at the Council meeting of 22 June 2024 , including failures to disclose legal opinions and financial implications known to Exco members . <p> 16.12 . The letter , additionally , reveals that Council had no idea of the adverse impact its resolutions would have on the DGF hospital donation to UCT . <p> 17 . On 6 September 2024 , Ms Kelly Phelps , the twenty-seventh respondent , filed a notice to abide . She reserved her right to file an affidavit " for the purpose of placing any facts germane to this application so as to assist the Court " . <p> 18 . On 11 September 2024 , FWB came on record for the Chair of Council ( not Council itself ) and UCT ( cited as the thirty-second respondent ) . <p> 19 . On 13 September 2024 , the Chair and UCT purported to file a record in terms of rule 53 . I annex the index ( SFA3 ) . I shall discuss the contents of this purported record in the next part of this affidavit . For now , I underscore three points . <p> 19.1 . First , it was unclear to me why the Chair and UCT had filed the record . I have impugned Council 's decision . FWB had not come on record for Council ( but only for the Chair and UCT ) . So , the wrong functionaries had filed a record , and Council had failed to file a record . Most importantly , given that Council was only scheduled to meet after 30 September 2024 , it appeared that Council had not been consulted about filing this record . The Chair seems unilaterally to have decided on the contents of the record . <p> 19.2 . Second , the purported record patently included documents that were not before Council before it took its decision , such as a campus announcement ( of 24 June 2024 ) postdating the decision . <p> 19.3 . Third , the purported record failed to include a transcript or recording of Council 's meeting of 22 June 2024 . The minutes revealed that members participated in the meeting via Microsoft Teams . The meeting would therefore have been recorded , but there was no inclusion of this recording in the purported record . <p> 20 . Council should have filed a record in terms of rule 53 by no later than 16 September 2024 . It failed to do so . <p> 21 . On 17 September 2024 , I wrote to Council ( SFA4 ) . <p> 21.1 . I explained that Council was yet to file a record . I afforded Council until 23 September 2024 to file its record . <p> 21.2 . I requested that when Council files its record it provides , as required by rule 53 , ( a ) a recording of its meeting of 22 June 2024 , and ( b ) only those documents that served before it . <p> 22 . Also on 17 September 2024 , News24 published two op-eds on my application . <p> The one supports my application ( SFAS ) and the other does not ( SFA6 ) . I attach these articles to reiterate a point I made in my founding affidavit and as being relevant to Council 's policy on when to issue public statements . The issues arising from Israel 's war against Hamas in Gaza are polarising , complex , and subject to robust debate . Reasonable people in good faith disagree about Israeli and Palestinian issues . Accordingly , those issues are not of the kind that Council can pronounce on - certainly not by precluding academics from " having relations with " other academics . <p> 23 . On 19 September 2024 , FWB on behalf of the Chair and UCT sent a letter in which two important things are said ( SFA7 ) . <p> 23.1 . First , they tender a transcript of the relevant portion of the Council meeting . This is important because the Chair later reneged on this tender - twice - with no justification . I address this below . <p> 23.2 . Second , the Chair and UCT explained that their purported record " contains all information relevant to the impugned decision or proceedings " . This is important because , as I explain below , the Chair , Council , and UCT later concede that all these documents ( save one page ) were not before Council when it took its decision . They concede , in other words , that these " relevant " documents were not before Council . <p> 24 . On 20 September 2024 , I followed up with Council ( SFAB ) . I requested confirmation of a date by which Council would file its record . I appreciated that Council is a deliberative body comprising thirty persons , so I offered Council an extension ( until 25 September 2024 ) to file its record . <p> 25 . Also on 20 September 2024 , I responded to the Chair and UCT ( SFA9 ) . I thanked them for their tender of a transcript . I explained however that the recording and transcript are a matter for Council to decide . <p> 26 . On 25 September 2024 , UCT 's Registrar , in his capacity as Council 's secretary , informed me that a special meeting of Council has been scheduled for 5 October 2024 , followed by an ordinary meeting on 19 October 2024 . During these meetings , Council would deliberate on my application ( SFA10 ) . <p> 27 . On 26 September 2024 , I issued two notices . <p> 27.1 . First , a rule 7(1) notice disputing FWB 's authority to act only for the Chair of Council ( SFA11 ) . I also disputed the Chair 's authority to represent Council , to the extent that he did so . I further disputed FWB 's authority to represent UCT . ( FWB later duly provided its authority to represent UCT . ) <p> 27.2 . Second , a rule 30A notice requiring Council to comply with rule 53 and file a record ( SFA12 ) . <p> 28 . On 30 September 2024 , the South African Union for Jewish Students , a student organisation representing Jewish students at universities across South Africa , wrote a letter to Council objecting to the resolutions ( SFA13 ) . <p> 28.1 . The letter expressed support for this application . <p> 28.2 . The letter demonstrates how the resolutions contravene UCT 's Risk Management Policy of June 2020 . I attach the Policy as SFA14 . When dealing with my grounds of review below , I discuss the provisions of the Risk Management Policy and how Council 's decision was taken contrary to its provisions . <p> 28.3 . The letter also points out that Council failed to consider and adhere to the Regulations for Reporting by Public Higher Education Institutions published under GN R464 in GG 37726 of 9 June 2014 . I return to this below too . <p> ( ii ) October 2024 <p> 29 . On 2 October 2024 , Professor Glenda Gray - a professor at the University of the Witwatersrand and a member of Council as from July 2024 - was appointed chair of the board of the Global Antibiotic Research &amp; Development Partnership ( GARDP ) . Her appointment is relevant to this application because it demonstrates the severe and irrational reach of the resolutions passed by Council . <p> 29.1 . GARDP is a not-for-profit global health organisation , established by the World Health Organisation , driven to protect people from the rise and spread of drug-resistant infections . Through public and private partnerships , GARDP develops and makes antibiotic treatments accessible for people who need them . GARDP is highly regarded for its work in global health . <p> 29.2 . GARDP includes academics from around the world in its various programmes . One of those academics is Professor Mica ! Paul , an Israeli academic at the Faculty of Medicine , Technion , Israel Institute of Technology ( SFA15 ) . So , arguably , Professor Gray has " entered relations " with " the broader Israeli military establishment " or with a network whose affiliations include members of the Israeli military . Had she therefore been a UCT rather than a Wits professor she could have been precluded by Council 's resolutions from chairing GARDP . <p> 29.3 . Additionally , in 2023 , Professor Gray , in her capacity as President and CEO of the South African Medical Research Council , joined the Human Frontier Science Program ( HFSP ) . <p> 29.4 . This should be celebrated . The HFSP was founded in 1989 to advance international research and training at the frontier of the life sciences . Its aims are to promote intercontinental collaboration and training in cutting-edge , interdisciplinary research focused on the life sciences . With its collaborative research grants and postdoctoral fellowships , the HFSP has issued over 4 500 awards involving more than 7 500 scientists from all over the world . Since the beginning of the HFSP , 29 HFSP awardees have gone on to win the Nobel Prize . <p> 29.5 . But there is a catch . The HFSP receives funding from Israel ( along with 15 other countries and the European Union ) . Israel joined the HFSP in 2019 . The HFSP said in December 2023 : " Israel is one of the most recent HFSP members ( 2019 ) , however , Israeli scientific research is remarkable and highly supported by HFSP ; Israeli scientists and scientific institutions have received numerous HFSP Research Grants , Fellowships , and the prestigious HFSP Nakasone Award . This week , we are here to reinforce this collaboration between Israel , through its governmental and scientific institutions , and HFSPO . We both want to pursue excellence in scientific development " ( SFA16 ) . <p> 29.6 . Also in December 2023 , Professor Gray co-hosted the HFSP in Cape Town - an event that included Israeli members of the HFSP Council and Board of Trustees . <p> 29.7 . Council 's decision jeopardises any interactions with the HFSP by UCT academics . Since the HFSP celebrates and promotes Israeli scientific research , collaborating , hosting , or interacting with the HFSP constitutes entering relations with what Council may consider the " broader Israeli military establishment " . It may well include interacting with research groups or a network with authors " affiliated " with the IDF . <p> 29.8 . Thus the resolutions adopted by Council would potentially preclude UCT academics from obtaining accolades and career-advancing international positions similar to those obtained by a member of Council . Council has seen fit to impose a high cost on academics at UCT , a cost which the majority of Council members , not being UCT academics , do not themselves have to pay . <p> 30 . On 4 October 2024 , FWB on behalf of the Chair and UCT responded to my rule 7(1) notice ( SFA17 ) . <p> 30.1 . FWB averred that it had authority to represent the Chair because the Chair was in possession of the record of Council 's decision , and because the Registrar had instructed FWB to come on record for the Chair . <p> 30.2 . The Chair averred that he did not need authority from Council to represent Council in this litigation " by virtue of his position as the Chairperson of the Council , by operation of law and as recognised under Rule 53 " . <p> 31 . On 7 October 2024 , I responded by pointing out various issues with FWB 's and the Chair 's authority arising from the letter of 4 October 2024 ( SFA18 ) . <p> 31.1 . First , the Chair alleged that he had not " acted on his own behalf or ' as an official distinct from the Council " ' ( paragraph 2 ) . He also alleged that he " has acted only in an official and representative capacity as the Chairperson of the Council " ( paragraph 3 , emphasis added ) . These statements are inconsistent . The Chair can not act only as Chair and not act as an official distinct from Council . <p> 31.2 . Second , the Chair can only represent Council if Council decides to authorise him to do so . Rule 53 does not clothe the Chair with authority to represent Council in these proceedings without Council 's permission ( and neither does any other law ) . Of course , if the Chair has Council 's authorisation , then he may take steps under rule 53 on behalf of Council . But the Chair failed to provide any evidence of Council 's authorisation . <p> 31.3 . Third , FWB had previously advised , in its letter of 19 September 2024 , that it represents only the Chair - expressly not Council . So , it is apparent that the Chair was not authorised by Council to represent Council - he was acting on his own . <p> 31.4 . Fourth , the Registrar had no authority to instruct FWB to represent the Chair- only Council could do so . It therefore appeared that FWB was representing the Chair without proper authority . <p> 32 . Also on 7 October 2024 , I wrote to Council following up on the outcome of its meeting of 5 October 2024 ( SFA19 ) . Council responded on the same day , saying that it will only consider the contents of the record filed by the Chair on 19 October 2024 ( SFA20 ) . No reason was given for this inordinate delay in filing the record , and for Council 's breach of duty to this Court under rule 53 . <p> 33 . On 9 October 2024 , Professor Pierre de Vos , an academic at UCT and a member of the Academic Freedom Committee ( AFC ) , wrote an article accusing me of " lawfare " and the " Mpofu-fication " of UCT by bringing this application ( SFA21 ) . While Professor de Vos purports to criticise this application ( again , demonstrating the polarising nature of the resolutions ' subject-matter ) , he makes various allegations that support this application . For instance , he explains : <p> 33.1 . " Public universities do not ' belong ' to any specific group , but serve society as a whole and should aim to become places where all students can ' belong ' , by which I mean flourish and succeed . " <p> 33.2 . " The ideal university is not a place where people are marginalised and excluded because of their race or because they are not the kind of people who thrive in a system that values group identity and loyalty to the collective above all else . In my view , universities ( and the academics working there ) also serve the public good by engaging critically with socioeconomic problems and other injustices they see in the world around them " ( emphasis added ) . <p> 33.3 . " To be honest , I am not sure I would have supported Council 's decision in the face of compelling evidence that it would lead to a catastrophic loss of hundreds of millions of rands in subsidies , donations and grants for the university . " <p> As it happens , apparently unbeknown to Professor de Vos , not only is there incontrovertible evidence that UCT will lose at least hundreds of millions of rand , but also ( a ) this evidence was deliberately not disclosed at the Council meeting and ( b ) Council never even considered this evidence . <p> 34 . On 10 October 2024 , FWB and the Chair purported to address the issues with their authority raised by my rule 7(1) notice ( SFA22 ) . <p> 34.1 . The Chair repeated his mistaken view that he never " required specific authorisation from the Council to produce the rule 53 record of the proceedings over which he presided " . That the Chair thinks he can act without Council 's authorisation is , with respect , startling . <p> 34.2 . FWB failed entirely to address the issue with its authority , namely that the Chair and the Registrar had no authority from Council to instruct its offices . <p> 34.3 . In an apparent concession that neither the Chair nor FWB had the requisite authority , the letter recorded that Council at its meeting of 19 October 2024 would " confirm " the Chair 's and FWB 's authority . <p> 35 . On 10 October 2024 , I agreed to allow Council until 23 October 2024 to file its record , given that it was meeting on 19 October 2024 ( SFA23 ) . I expressly reserved my rights , however . Notwithstanding my indulgences , Council owes a duty to this Court to file its record . It was in breach of this duty without any explanation . <p> 36 . On 16 October 2024 , the Vice-Chancellor " put on hold " his annual Open Lecture ( SFA24 ) . The Vice-Chancellor " paused " the lecture because the invited speaker was considered by some members of the UCT community to be supportive of Israel . The Vice-Chancellor explained : " The lecture would have had a polarising effect on campus . It therefore became important to pause the event and allow for further discussions to take place without the time pressure . " The inconsistency is patent . The Vice-Chancellor was prepared to " pause " and conduct discussions before taking a final decision on an Israel-related *Issue . Council , on the other hand , passed a " polarising " resolution without any community engagement or consultation . <p> 37 . On 17 October 2024 , the news site Politicsweb published a report dated January 2019 f1tled " UCT : The Cost of Cancelling Israel " ( SFA25 ) . I shall call this the Risk Assessment Report . I return to its contents below , when explaining why the failure to consider this report is fatal to Council 's decision . <p> 38 . Also on 17 October 2024 , UCT 's Registrar informed members of Council that Council had voted not to release the audio recording of its meeting of 22 June 2024 as part of the rule 53 record ( SFA26 ) . <p> 39 . On 19 October 2024 , Council met ( SFA27 ) . The minutes of its meeting have two relevant parts . <p> 40 . First , a council member ( Professor Moultrie ) raised the issue of Exco 's failure to disclose the risk of losing millions of rands in donations at the meeting of 22 June 2024 . <p> 40.1 . Professor Moultrie " argued that the interim Vice-Chancellor was not truthful about the risks associated with the Dell Foundation and DGF , framing them as mere risks when they were actual material risks " ( emphasis added ) . Professor Moultrie submitted to Council that the failure to disclose the donations before voting on the resolutions amounted to a " significant governance crisis " . <p> 40.3 . The Vice-Chancellor ( now the incumbent , who took office on 1 August 2024 ) suggested that his predecessor may not have considered the risk of losing the DGF and Dell Foundation donations as material because DGF only indicated that UCT was in breach on 1 August 2024 . <p> 40.4 . This is factually incorrect . The minutes of the Exco meeting of 23 May 2024 record that the ( then ) Vice-Chancellor informed Exco that DGF had " indicated that UCT was in breach of the donation agreement " . Moreover , by the time of that Exco meeting the Dell Foundation had already decided to suspend funding . The risk was not only material , but it had also materialised . <p> 40.5 . The Chair informed Council - for the first time - that he had met with representatives of DGF after the 23 May 2024 Exco meeting and before the 22 June 2024 meeting . So , not only had the Chair failed to disclose to Council the risk of losing the DGF funding , but he had also failed to inform Council that he had met with those donors , and that those donors had expressed concerns to him about Council adopting the resolutions . <p> 40.6 . Four Council members ( Ms Mohamed , Mr Moollatjie , Professor Amien , and Mr Campbell ) suggested that Council had been aware of the risk of losing the R200 million DGF donation ( there is no mention , I underscore , of the millions from the Dell Foundation ) . But this is belied by the rule 53 record . There was nothing before Council alluding to the risk of losing the DGF and Dell Foundation donations ; nor was this mentioned by any of the Exco members , or by any other member of Council , during the meeting of 22 June 2024 . And the letter of 5 September 2024 from several Council members confirms that Council was not even aware of the existence of the DGF donation contract ( and therefore of its terms ) . <p> 40.7 . Some Council members suggested that donors have attempted to " bully " or " strong-arm " UCT . This misses the point , in three respects . First , the point is not whether a donor 's demand is reasonable . The point is that Council , before taking any decision , which must be in the best interests of the university , should know the financial implications of its decision . In this case , it simply did not . Second , UCT 's ongoing compliance with the requirement that it continue with its policy of tolerance for differences of religion , culture and so forth , including zero tolerance for antisemitism as defined by the IHRA , was a term of the DGF donation contract signed by the Chair and the then Vice ? Chancellor and therefore pre-dated the Council resolutions . There can be no suggestion of UCT 's being " bullied " or " strong-armed " where it breached the terms of an existing contract to which it agreed . Third , donations are by their nature charitable . Donors are under no obligation to donate to any particular institution . If donors are discomfited by the policies of an institution , for whatever reason , they are fully within their rights rather to donate elsewhere ( or not to donate at all ) . <p> 40.8 . The contract of donation referred to in paragraph 52.5 of the founding affidavit is the one with DGF . There I said that the contract had been signed on behalf of UCT in September 2023 by the then Vice ? Chancellor and by the Chair . I have since learned that a slightly amended version of the contract was signed on behalf of UCT on 17 November 2023 , again by the then Vice-Chancellor and by the Chair ( the amendment concerned a change of name to the DGF ) . Between the two signature dates , the attack of 7 October 2023 on Israel had taken place and the war in Gaza had commenced . By mid-November it was well known that those events had resulted in significant divisions among members of the UCT community . Yet the Chair apparently had no difficulty with signing a contract that obliged UCT to conduct itself with due regard for the IHRA definition of antisemitism . <p> 40.9 . Council resolved to appoint a subcommittee to investigate the failure to disclose the risk of losing the donations to Council . Curiously , the minutes record that only the Registrar was recused from commenting on the subcommittee 's terms of reference and considering the subcommittee 's findings . The Exco members , who are directly implicated by the allegation of non-disclosure , appear to have had a say in the terms of reference of the sub-committee . This is a plain and simple conflict of interest . <p> 41 . Second , the minutes record a report provided by the Vice-Chancellor on the impact of Council 's " Gaza/Israel " resolutions . <p> 41.1 . Regarding financial impact , the Vice-Chancellor reported that : ( a ) the resolutions have cost the university an estimated amount of R250 million in withdrawals ; ( b ) this amount excludes future or potential losses , including how donors ( counting major donors like Wendy Ackermann , the Dell Foundation , and the Harry Crossley Foundation ) have said they may no longer provide funding going forward ; and ( c ) donors have expressed concern with the second of Council 's resolutions , which prohibits " relations " between academics . <p> 41.2 . The Vice-Chancellor revealed that UCT 's entire postgraduate funding annual investment is R400 million . So , the resolutions have cost the university an amount equivalent to over half that budget - not even counting future and potential losses of donations , as well as potentially larger of losses of research grants . <p> 41.3 . The Vice-Chancellor 's report does not mention the jeopardised R500 million donation from the DGF to UCT for a private academic hospital . So , while the Vice-Chancellor mentions R250 million in withdrawals , the number is far higher ( and not even known to the Vice-Chancellor ) . The number , to my knowledge , considering the scuppered DGF hospital donation , is closer to R750 million . <p> 41.4 . As for the impact on students and staff , the Vice-Chancellor reported that his consultations have revealed a " divided climate " at UCT and " a sense of discomfort and fear among the community " . He warned that the resolutions may " jeopardise UCT 's academic integrity and reputation " , and that the situation was " worsening " . He highlighted the risk of " losing talented individuals to other prestigious institutions if the environment became toxic " . <p> 41.5 . Council members responded to the Vice-Chancellor 's report . There are three important themes arising from these responses . <p> 41.6 . First , there is an acknowledgement that Council must consider the financial implications of its decisions before taking those decisions . Council members , now in hindsight , acknowledged how they should have considered all financial upshots to Council 's resolutions before deciding to pass those resolutions . Even the Chair ( who failed to disclose the risk to funding at the meeting of 22 June 2024 ) " raised concerns about how the current situation , including potential funding losses , might impact UCT 's reputation " ( emphasis added ) . <p> 41.7 . Second , Council members expressed concern about the ongoing tensions on campus , and suggested that the way forward was " understanding and dialogue " to mitigate polarisation . <p> 41.8 . Third , members flagged Council 's and Senate 's focus on political issues at the cost of governance and academic issues . The Vice ? Chancellor and the Registrar ( in addition to several Council members ) warned that Council and Senate are too focused on external issues , instead of dedicating their time to addressing issues within the university . <p> 41.9 . I note a further aspect of the responses . At various points , certain Council members suggested that UCT consider obtaining funding from " non-Western " sources , including the Gulf States and BRICS countries . As I pointed out in my founding affidavit ( paragraph 72 ) , there is an obvious problem with these sources of funding ( which members of Council did not appear to appreciate ) . These countries <p> - certainly Russia , Iran , and Saudi Arabia ( all members of BRICS ) - stand accused of serious international legal breaches . If Council can stymie academic relations with Israel or Israeli academics , then it can also do so for academic relations with those countries . And not stymieing relations with these countries , while doing so for Israel , is ( at least ) irrational . In any event , Council has no immediate relationship with any of these " non-Western sources " to readily replace the R750 million it has lost because of its resolutions . <p> 41.10 . The minutes record that Council discussed this application under a " confidential agenda " . I understand that during that discussion Council decided to oppose my application , with 16 votes in favour and 10 votes against . <p> 42 . On 21 October 2024 , Council announced that it had resolved to oppose this application ( SFA28 ) . This is inexplicable . Council decided to oppose this application ( a ) before filing a rule 53 record , ( b ) before seeing my supplementary founding affidavit , and ( c ) despite Council being divided ( 16 to 10 ) on the merits of my application . This is an irrational expenditure of university funds . Those who voted to oppose my application breached their fiduciary duties in doing so . <p> 43 . On 23 October 2024 , FWB wrote to me now on behalf of the Chair , Council , and UCT ( SFA29 ) . The letter informed me that at its meeting of 19 October 2024 , Council had decided to - <p> 43.1. " ratify " the Registrar 's decision to instruct FWB to act on behalf of the Chairperson and UCT ; <p> 43.2. authorise FWB and the Chair to act on behalf of Council in these proceedings ; <p> 43.3. " ratify " the Chair 's decision to dispatch the rule 53 record ; and <p> 43.4. oppose this application . <p> 44 . This letter confirms that the Chair , the Registrar , and FWB failed to act with Council 's authority until 19 October 2024 . It also confirms that Council ( now ) considered the purported record filed by the Chair as the record of its decision . <p> 45 . On 24 October 2024 , I wrote to the Chair , Council , and UCT ( SFA30 ) . I made three requests . <p> 45.1 . First , I requested reasons for Council 's decision to oppose my application . Without reasons , Council 's decision to oppose implies that Council is not committed to lawful and rational decision-making . If it were so committed , Council would have waited to see a// the grounds on which I challenge its decision based on a full and complete record of its proceedings . Instead , unless Council explains its decision to oppose , the implication is that Council is committed to defending the impugned resolutions , at all costs , no matter the grounds for reviewing that decision . <p> 45.2 . Second , I requested that Council clarify which of the " relevant " documents filed in the purported rule 53 record were before it when it decided to pass the resolutions . <p> 45.3 . Third , now that FWB represented Council , I accepted the tender for the transcript of the relevant part of the Council meeting . <p> 46 . On 29 October 2024 , I followed up with Council regarding my requests <p> ( SFA31 ) . <p> 47 . On 31 October 2024 , Council responded ( SFA32 ) . <p> 47.1 . First , it refused to explain why it has opposed this application without seeing my supplementary founding affidavit , before finalising its rule 53 record , and despite the dissensus among its members . Council alleged that its majority took a " considered " decision to oppose - but this can not be . Council could not have considered the contents of this affidavit , and all the contents of the rule 53 record , which should be considered by an organ of state before deciding to oppose ( and thereby expend public funds ) . Hence rule 53 itself and my notice of motion provide for the filing of a notice of opposition after the filing of the supplementary founding affidavit . <p> 47.2 . Second , it conceded the merits of this application . Council admitted that the only documents that served before Council were " those identified in item 5 of the Rule 53 record ( paginated page 90 ) " . Item 5 of the record consists of one page , and is described in the index to the record as " Extracts of the Agenda of Meeting of the Council of the University " , further identified as the meeting of 22 June 2024 . In turn , that single page identifies two relevant documents , namely the two Senate resolutions ultimately adopted by Council ( SFA33 and SFA34 ) . That is it . The only relevant documents before Council were the resolutions . None of the other documents in the filed " record " were before Council . <p> 47.3 . Council 's admission concedes the merits of this application . In its letter of 19 September 2024 ( as ratified on 19 October 2024 ) , Council explained that the rule 53 record it filed " contains all information relevant to the impugned decision or proceedings " . Council then admitted that of all that relevant information , none was before it when it took its decision other than the two resolutions referred to in the page extracted from the agenda . Council , on its own version , ignored " all information relevant to " its decision . <p> 47.4 . Third , Council now refused to provide a transcript of the meeting ( despite the previous , open tender which I had accepted ) . Council asserted that it would only provide a " redacted " transcript , in WhiCh the names of Council members will be hidden . Its reasons : ( a ) * ' the impugned resolutions raise sensitive and highly politicrsed Issues that can provoke extreme reactions " , and ( b ) " indiVidual Council members may be exposed to victimisation and intimidation if their individual contributions in the deliberations are exposed " . <p> 47.5 .
